@@26304083 <h> FOLLOW PHOTOS <h> ' Major transformation ' ahead for Earth 's ecosystems : study <p> Some of these changes are already under way in the southwestern United States , where massive wildfires are destroying pine forests and transforming swaths of territory into shrubland. n the next 100-150 years , these changes will likely extend to savannas , deserts , and woodlands . <p> AFP Updated : Aug 31 , 2018 , 13:09 IST <p> In this file photo taken on August 3 , 2018 flames estimated by firefighters to be 60-80 metres in height ( 200- ... Read More <p> TAMPA : The Earth 's forests , deserts , landscapes and vital ecosystems risk a " major transformation " in the next century due to climate change , international scientists warned on Thursday . <p> Some of these changes are already under way in the southwestern United States , where massive wildfires are destroying pine forests and transforming swaths of territory into shrubland . <p> In the next 100-150 years , these changes will likely extend to savannas , deserts , and woodlands , upsetting ecosystems and imperiling plant and animal life , particularly in areas like Europe @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Science . <p> " If we allow climate change to go unchecked , the vegetation of this planet is going to look completely different than it does today , and that means a huge risk to the diversity of the planet , " said co-author Jonathan Overpeck , dean of the School for Environment and Sustainability at the University of Michigan . <p> The report is based on fossil and temperature records from a period that began 21,000 years ago , when the last Ice Age ended and the planet warmed seven to 13 degrees Fahrenheit ( 4-7 degrees Celsius ) . <p> But experts say their predictions are conservative , since this historical warming , caused by natural variability , took place over a much longer period -- from the Last Glacial Maximum 21,000 years ago until the early Holocene , about 10,000 years ago . <p> But human-caused climate change is different . The burning of fossil fuels like oil and coal emits heat-trapping gases around the planet . The Earth is currently heating up at much quicker pace . <p> " We 're talking about the same @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to be crammed into a century or two , " said Stephen Jackson , director of the US Geological Survey 's Southwest Climate Adaptation Center . <p> " Ecosystems are going to be scrambling to catch up . " <p> Researchers described their work as the most comprehensive study to date , based on pollen and plant-fossil records from 594 sites worldwide , dating back to between 21,000 and 14,000 years ago . <p> Every continent except Antarctica was included . <p> The most significant changes were seen in the mid-to-high latitudes of North America , Europe and southern South America . These were regions that had been most covered in glaciers , and which warmed up the most as the climate shifted . <p> Researchers calculated that if little is done to contain current emissions , " the probability of large-scale vegetation change is greater than 60 percent . " <p> However , if greenhouse gasses are limited to levels targeted in the 2015 Paris Agreement , " the probability of large-scale vegetation change is less than 45 percent . @@26308768 <h> Columnists <h> Bay of opportunity <p> Bimstec summit will go a long way in promoting regional cooperation at a time when fora like Saarc are ineffective <p> The Bay of Bengal Initiative for Multi-sectoral Technical and Economic Cooperation ( Bimstec ) meet in Kathmadu that concluded on August 31 , saw a significant show of strength from member countries whose top leaders were in attendance . Bound by common hopes and universal aspirations , the leaders gathered to address the challenges towards attaining prosperity for the region . Some of the broad contours that were discussed and debated during the 4th Bimstec summit were poverty alleviation , elimination and better management of natural disasters that almost all member nations have been through in recent times and increasing connectivity for mutually beneficial trade , transport and digital initiatives . Indeed , the seven-nation grouping of Bangladesh , Bhutan , India , Sri Lanka , Thailand , Myanmar and Nepal has come a long way from being just a low-profile regional bloc of five nations established in 1997 to a grouping that member countries increasingly view as a viable platform to take forward their regional , strategic and economic interests @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and stagnation of a forum such as Saarc mainly due to an incorrigible Pakistan with its terrorism , radical Islamism and Kashmir priorities which has made the South Asian regional grouping pretty ineffective and escalated bilateral tensions , promoting Bimstec makes a lot of strategic sense for India . There is significant overlap , to be sure , between other regional groupings and Bimstec , five of whose members are also part of Saarc , two of Asean and six of the SASEC . But we are not suggesting Bimstec as an alternative to these regional groupings but as a vital , supplementary sub-regional organisation that will help its members leverage their interests in larger fora too . Every regional body has its own vision and challenges but the broader vision for the South Asian and Southeast Asian regions must be worked on through various avenues and eliding recalcitrant nations and/or their backers . The breakthrough for Bimstec came by way of a mini-summit held in Goa on the sidelines of the Brics summit in 2016 , where some top priority areas like terrorism , disaster management , climate change @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ meaningful cooperation . It is India 's interest to build on it . 
@@26310513 <h> Share Via Email <p> CHENNAI : The Madhouse Effect , is a book by award-winning climate scientist Michael E Mann , and the Pulitzer Prize winning political cartoonist Tom Toles . According to one review , " it portrays the intellectual pretzels into which denialists must twist logic to explain away the clear evidence that human activity has changed Earth 's climate " . Both have fought relentlessly against climate denialism for most of their careers . While some overlook warning signs that links wanton human actions to catastrophic changes in climate , it is impossible to ignore anymore the hand of climate change on catastrophes like the recent floods in Kerala . It is an example of multiple failures caused by implementation of unsustainable development plans , and dubbed the Western Ghats ' worst flood in a century . <p> After a two-week bout of non-stop rain , Kerala has been left ravaged by floods with many millions in relief camps and unknown numbers dead ; and worse , still trapped under old buildings where they were seeking refuge . While reports on Kerala have flooded the media , there is less talk of areas in Coorg @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ due to the same rain system . Exacerbated by landslides in hilly terrain , flooding has led to the destruction of homes , bridges , road networks , and industries . <p> In an era of climate uncertainties that we have just entered , extreme rainfall events are going to become increasingly common . Uncontrolled growth at the expense of the environment will severely amplify the impact of climate change , and surely seems stupid to deny anymore . Our cities are simply not prepared for extreme weather events . In the case of Kerala as well , these events were predicted , and the possibility of such devastation was highlighted several years ago . <p> In 2011 , the Western Ghats Ecology Expert Panel , chaired by the internationally-renowned ecologist Madhav Gadgil , submitted a report to the Indian Ministry of Environment and Forests . The report warned that an ill-thought focus on development was impacting the sustainability of the Western Ghats hill chain , one of the world 's most biodiverse areas that runs along the west coast of India . The expert report urged several states , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ conservation , and recommended the limiting of activities such as quarrying , and construction of dams near protected forests in hilly areas . The report was rejected by the Ministry as well as by both states . <p> Dismissing the warning signs , tens of thousands of trees were felled in Kodagu district of the Western Ghats in Karnataka in 2015 to construct high tension electric lines . Sand mining of river beds went unchecked and high-rise buildings were sanctioned on unstable hill slopes . These decisions have weakened the soil making it prone to landslides . The problem of course is much more complex . <p> Now urban and forest zones of Kerala and Karnataka are susceptible to flash floods , soil erosion , and even earthquakes . Should there be a chance to influence or vote on any development , irrespective of scale , be always cognizant of the cause and effect theory at play . We can not be a party to the mindless drive to develop because there is a future cost to legate too . <h> Comments <p> Disclaimer : We respect your thoughts and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ your comments .
